Coalition of 16 Attorneys General Widens Lawsuit Over CDC's Hepatitis B Change as a Model Projects a Sharp Rise in Childhood Infections - Nature World News

News Brief
A coalition of 16 state attorneys general and Pennsylvania's governor has widened a lawsuit against the CDC's decision to change the hepatitis B vaccination policy, arguing that the policy change violates the Administrative Procedure Act and that the CDC's replacement of all 17 voting committee members in June 2025 was unlawful. The legal action is based on a study published on October 19, 2026, in the Annals of Internal Medicine, which projected that moving the first dose from birth to a family decision could raise chronic hepatitis B infections in children by 83% by age 20. The study simulated a birth cohort of 3.6 million U.S. infants and found that delaying the first dose increased the risk of chronic infections by 19%. The CDC's website still recommends the hepatitis B vaccine for all infants, and a federal court in Massachusetts has stayed the January 2026 CDC memo. The U.S. Court of Appeals for the First Circuit heard arguments on October 29, 2026, but no appellate ruling had been reported. The study is a peer-reviewed computer model, not a count of real infections, and its results depend on assumptions about vaccine uptake and exposure. The legal and policy changes were made in 2025, with the January 2026 CDC memo removing the universal recommendation for seven childhood vaccines, including hepatitis B. The states argue that these changes could lead to a significant increase in the risk of lifelong chronic hepatitis B, which raises the risk of liver cirrhosis and liver cancer decades later. The CDC's decision has been paused by a federal court, and the Justice Department has appealed the ruling. The hepatitis B vaccine remains covered for children through Vaccines for Children and most insurance plans.
Original source text (English)
Home Animals Biology Environment Health & Medicine Tech Travel Space Natureworldnews.com Let's Connect Trending climate change Earth whales Coalition of 16 Attorneys General Widens Lawsuit Over CDC’s Hepatitis B Change as a Model Projects a Sharp Rise in Childhood Infections By Avery Porter Published: Oct 29 2026, 12:26 PM EDT Facebook Twitter Linkedin Comment Mail ADEK BERRY | AFP via Getty Images The legal fight over the federal government's decision to drop a universal hepatitis B vaccine dose at birth is widening. Vermont joined a multistate lawsuit this month, bringing the coalition to 16 state attorneys general plus Pennsylvania's governor, after the states filed an amended complaint on Oct. 2, according to the Vermont attorney general's office. The case arrives alongside new evidence. A study published Oct. 19 in Annals of Internal Medicine projected that moving the first dose from birth to a family decision could raise chronic hepatitis B infections in children by 83% by age 20, according to a release from the American College of Physicians. Under that scenario, more than half of children could enter adulthood without protection. For parents of newborns, the practical question is simpler than the legal one. Because a federal court has paused the change, the CDC's website still says all infants should receive the hepatitis B vaccine, ABC News reported. The American Academy of Pediatrics continues to recommend a first dose within 24 hours of birth. Two Court Fights, One Policy In December 2025, the Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices voted to end the universal birth-dose recommendation in place since 1991. The CDC then adopted shared clinical decision-making for babies whose mothers test negative, meaning parents and providers decide together whether to give the dose at birth or start no earlier than 2 months, according to the Department of Health and Human Services. A January 2026 CDC memo went further, removing the universal recommendation for seven childhood vaccines, including hepatitis B. The states argue in federal court in Northern California that these changes violate the Administrative Procedure Act and that Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.'s replacement of all 17 voting committee members in June 2025 was unlawful. Vermont Attorney General Charity Clark said the changes "run afoul of decades of scientific evidence." A separate case brought by the American Academy of Pediatrics produced the ruling now in effect. On March 16, U.S. District Judge Brian Murphy in Massachusetts stayed the January memo, the new committee appointments and the committee's votes, including the hepatitis B decision. The Justice Department appealed on April 29, STAT reported, and the U.S. Court of Appeals for the First Circuit heard arguments on Oct. 6. As of Oct. 29, no appellate ruling had been reported. The Administration's Case for Choice HHS has defended the change as a matter of informed consent for low-risk families. Babies born to mothers who test positive or whose status is unknown are still recommended to receive the birth dose with immunoglobulin. When the policy was adopted, then-acting CDC Director Jim O'Neill said those newborns whose mothers test negative "face little risk of contracting hepatitis B." Asked about the new study, HHS pointed ABC News to a post by Kennedy. "I reject the idea that respecting parents' choices means abandoning children's health," he wrote. HHS has also said insurance coverage for the vaccine remains in place across federal programs, including Vaccines for Children and Medicaid. What the Model Projects Researchers at Massachusetts General Hospital and Harvard Medical School followed a simulated birth cohort of 3.6 million U.S. infants through age 20. They compared three approaches for babies of mothers who test negative: a universal birth dose, a first dose delayed to 2 months, and shared decision-making for that first dose. Delaying the first dose raised projected chronic infections by 19%, while shared decision-making raised them by 83%. The number of children still vulnerable to infection at age 20 rose from about 232,000 under the universal birth dose to about 1.8 million under shared decision-making, according to ABC News. If high-risk babies were also vaccinated less reliably, infections passed from mother to child could double or triple. Corresponding author Dr. Amir Mohareb, an infectious-disease physician at Mass General Brigham, said the team aimed to add evidence to a debate already playing out in courts and professional societies. He called for restoring the universal birth-dose recommendation. Limits of a Projection The study is a peer-reviewed computer model, not a count of real infections. Its results depend on assumptions about vaccine uptake, maternal screening, exposure in households and missed follow-up doses, and the release did not report absolute numbers of new chronic infections. It also modeled a single year of births, so the authors' warning that effects would add up over many years is an extrapolation. What is not in dispute is the stakes of infection — and how early they begin. Most babies infected at birth or in infancy develop lifelong chronic hepatitis B, which has no cure and raises the risk of liver cirrhosis and liver cancer decades later. The vaccine is up to 90% effective at preventing mother-to-child infection when given within 24 hours of birth, the Vermont Attorney General's Office noted. For expectant parents, the most useful step now is to ask about prenatal hepatitis B testing and discuss the birth dose with their obstetrician or pediatrician before delivery. Families who skipped the birth dose can ask their pediatrician about starting the series. The hepatitis B vaccine remains covered for children through Vaccines for Children and most insurance plans. The First Circuit ruling is the next major event, and it will decide whether the March stay remains in place while the cases continue. The bottom line is that the universal birth dose is still the recommendation in effect today — but its future depends on appeals judges, and new modeling suggests the policy choice could shape liver health for millions of children decades from now. Related Deadly Hantavirus Outbreaks Could Rise Without a Hantavirus Vaccine, Experts Warn About Prevention Risks Early H1N1 Wave Pushes Mid-October Flu Hospitalization Rate to Its Highest Since 2010–11 Ebola Outbreak Caused by a Bat-Linked Virus With No Approved Vaccine Passes 4,000 Deaths in Congo What Readers Want to Know Is the hepatitis B birth dose still recommended? Yes, for now. A March 16 federal court order paused the change, and the CDC's website still says all infants should receive the vaccine. What did the CDC change? It moved the birth dose to shared clinical decision-making for babies whose mothers test negative, with a first dose no earlier than 2 months if the birth dose is skipped. Who is suing? A coalition of 16 state attorneys general and Pennsylvania's governor in federal court in Northern California, and the American Academy of Pediatrics in Massachusetts. What did the new study find? A model of 3.6 million infants projected 19% more chronic infections by age 20 with a delayed first dose and 83% more with shared decision-making. What does HHS say? HHS says the change respects parental choice for low-risk newborns and that insurance coverage for the vaccine continues. What should expectant parents do? Ask about prenatal hepatitis B testing and discuss the birth dose with your obstetrician or pediatrician before delivery. What happens next? The First Circuit, which heard arguments Oct. 6, will decide whether the court-ordered pause stays in place. © 2026 NatureWorldNews.com All rights reserved. Do not reproduce without permission. Time
The lawsuit was widened to include 16 state attorneys general and Pennsylvania's governor, with the amended complaint filed on October 2, 2026. The study was published on October 19, 2026, and the U.S. Court of Appeals for the First Circuit heard arguments on October 29, 2026. The legal and policy changes were made in 2025, with the January 2026 CDC memo removing the universal recommendation for seven childhood vaccines, including hepatitis B.
Location and population
The event involves the United States, specifically focusing on infants and children. The study simulated a birth cohort of 3.6 million U.S. infants through age 20, and the legal action is being pursued in federal court in Northern California.
Event
A coalition of 16 state attorneys general and Pennsylvania's governor has widened a lawsuit against the CDC's decision to change the hepatitis B vaccination policy. The lawsuit argues that the policy change violates the Administrative Procedure Act and that the CDC's replacement of all 17 voting committee members in June 2025 was unlawful. The legal action is based on a study projecting a sharp rise in childhood hepatitis B infections if the universal birth-dose recommendation is removed.
Scale, impact and risk
The study projected that shared decision-making for the first hepatitis B dose could raise chronic infections in children by 83% by age 20, increasing the number of vulnerable children from about 232,000 to about 1.8 million. The policy change could lead to a significant increase in the risk of lifelong chronic hepatitis B, which raises the risk of liver cirrhosis and liver cancer decades later.
Response, status and uncertainty
The CDC's website still recommends the hepatitis B vaccine for all infants, and a federal court in Massachusetts has stayed the January 2026 CDC memo. The study is a peer-reviewed computer model, not a count of real infections, and its results depend on assumptions about vaccine uptake and exposure.
